The issue describes a problem with markdown formatting where the second line of maximally nested lists has **extra whitespace**. 

**Technical Details:**
- **Expected output:** `        >       item` (8 spaces + ">" + 7 spaces + "item")  
- **Actual output:** `                >       item` (16 spaces + ">" + 7 spaces + "item")
- **Affected tests:** `test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_ordered_max_no_bq1` and `test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_unordered_max_no_bq1`
- **Context:** Related to issue #250, involves "maxed" nested structures

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Create a maximally nested markdown structure (block -> block -> ordered/unordered)
2. Process it through pymarkdown
3. Check the second line formatting - it should have 8 leading spaces but has 16

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Make minimal changes to core logic
- Focus on indentation calculation for nested blockquotes
- Ensure fix doesn't break other nested structures
- Test edge cases thoroughly

    22	[![LinkedIn](https://img.shields.io/badge/-LinkedIn-black.svg?logo=linkedin&colorB=555)](https://www.linkedin.com/in/jackdewinter/)
    23	
    24	PyMarkdown is primarily a Markdown Linter.  To ensure that the Markdown
    25	[linting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lint_%28software%29)
    26	is accomplished successfully, the rules engine that powers the linter
    27	uses a Markdown parser that is both
    28	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/)
    29	compliant and
    30	[CommonMark](https://spec.commonmark.org/)
    31	compliant.  The rules provided in the base application can be easily extended
    32	by writing new plugins and importing them into the rules engine through simple
    33	configuration options.
    34	
    35	The PyMarkdown project has the following advantages:
    36	
    37	- Consistency
    38	  - This project can examine multiple files and directories with one invocation,
    39	    ensuring that all detected Markdown files adhere to the same guidelines.
    40	- Portable
    41	  - The linter runs on any system running Python 3.8 or later, with no modifications.
    42	- Standardized
    43	  - The parser that powers the linter is GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM) compliant.
    44	    Due to that foundation, the parser does not guess how some parsers may handle
    45	    a given situation, as it has a clear set of rules to follow.
    46	- Accurate
    47	  - The parser passes all GFM conformance tests and CommonMark conformance tests.  In
    48	    test scenarios that were not present in either set of tests, the CommonMark
    49	    0.29.2 release was used to determine the correct parsing.
    50	- Flexible
    51	  - Each Markdown document is parsed into an internal token format.  Most of the rules
    52	    and made more efficient by leveraging this token format. Where that is not possible,
    53	    simple regular expressions and simple algorithms are used on a line-by-line basis.
    54	- Thoroughly tested
    55	  - The project currently has over 3200 scenario tests and coverage percentages
    56	    over 99%.
    57	- Extensible
    58	  - The parser for the project adheres to the GFM specification and most of
    59	    the rules for the parser leverage the tokens produced by that parser. The
    60	    [rules](/docs/rules.md) themselves are plugins, so they are extensible by default.
    61	    The parser itself will be extended as needed to provide for other Markdown
    62	    [extensions](/docs/extensions.md) as needed.
    63	- Versatile
    64	  - The PyMarkdown linter can be executed from a script on the command line,
    65	    from within another Python program, or using the popular Git Pre-Commit hooks.
    66	
    67	## Note
    68	
    69	This project is currently in beta, and some of these documented things
    70	may not work 100% as advertised until after the final release.  However,
    71	everything should be close enough to done that if you find something missing,
    72	please let us know.
    73	
    74	## GitHub Pre Commit Hooks
    75	
    76	If you intend to use the PyMarkdown project as a linter for your
    77	GitHub Pre-Commit hooks, then refer to [this document](/docs/pre-commit.md)
    78	on how to set that up.  Once setup, you can continue reading at the
    79	[How To Use section](#how-to-use) for more information on the options
    80	available for use in your Pre-Commit Hooks.
    81	
    82	## Requirements
    83	
    84	This project required Python 3.8 or later to function.

    86	## Installation
    87	
    88	```text
    89	pip install pymarkdownlnt
    90	```

    92	## How To Use
    93	
    94	### If You Get Stuck
    95	
    96	Full help support is available by entering
    97	
    98	```shell
    99	pymarkdown --help
   100	```
   101	
   102	on the command line and pressing enter.  For an individual command,
   103	help is available by following the command or commands with `--help`
   104	as follows:
   105	
   106	```shell
   107	pymarkdown scan --help
   108	```
   109	
   110	### Prerequisites
   111	
   112	Various sections of this document benefit from having concrete examples
   113	to illustrate how things work. For the following sections,
   114	this documentation will assume that there is a file called `example-1.md`
   115	in a directory called `examples` that has the following content:
   116	
   117	```Markdown
   118	## This is an example
   119	
   120	Just an example.
   121	```
   122	
   123	and a file called `example-2.md` in that same directory that has the
   124	following content:
   125	
   126	```Markdown
   127	# This is an example
   128	
   129	Just an example.
   130	
   131	```
   132	
   133	If you prefer concrete files, these files are checked into the
   134	[examples directory](https://github.com/jackdewinter/pymarkdown/tree/main/examples) of the GitHub project.
   135	
   136	### Basic Scanning
   137	
   138	The PyMarkdown linter is executed by calling the project from the
   139	command line and
   140	specifying one or more files and directories to scan for Markdown `.md`
   141	files.  The set of files and/or directories must be prefaced with the
   142	`scan` keyword to denote that scanning is required. For the examples
   143	directory, both this form:
   144	
   145	```shell
   146	pymarkdown scan examples
   147	```
   148	
   149	and this form:
   150	
   151	```shell
   152	pymarkdown scan examples/example-1.md examples/example-2.md
   153	```
   154	
   155	can be used to scan both files in the directory.  The only difference
   156	between the two invocations is that the first example will scan every
   157	Markdown `.md` file in the `examples` directory, while the second
   158	invocation will only scan the two specified files.  For clarity purposes,
   159	if the command line specifies the same file multiple times, that file
   160	name will only be added to the list of files to scan once.
   161	
   162	If everything is working properly, both of the above scans will
   163	produce the following output:
   164	
   165	```text
   166	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   167	```
   168	
   169	### Rules
   170	
   171	The PyMarkdown project includes 13 out-of-the-box [rules](/docs/rules.md),
   172	with another 29 rules to be added before the
   173	1.0.0 release.  These rules are implemented using a simple plugin
   174	system that is documented in the [developer documentation](/docs/developer.md).
   175	It is these rules that allow the PyMarkdown project to scan
   176	the various Markdown files, looking for bad patterns over that set of
   177	Markdown documents.
   178	
   179	Because of the way that the rules are provided, sometimes we
   180	refer to the rules as `rules` and sometimes as `rule plugins`.  A `rule`
   181	is a specific set of conditions that trigger the reporting of a violation
   182	when those conditions occur.  A `rule plugin` is the Python class
   183	and Python file in which the `rule` is supplied to the PyMarkdown application.
   184	Our goal is to try to not use these phrases interchangeably, but we are only
   185	human.  If we do mess up and use the wrong phase, we do apologize.
   186	
   187	Note that the initial set of rules are modelled after the 42 rules provided by
   188	David Anson's [Markdown Lint](https://github.com/markdownlint/markdownlint)
   189	project.  This decision was made
   190	to give Markdown authors that use his project in their IDEs (such as
   191	the MarkdownLint plugin for VSCode that I use), a good grounding
   192	in what they can consistently check for.
   193	
   194	### Rule Violation Format
   195	
   196	Executing either of the above example command lines will produce the following output:
   197	
   198	```text
   199	/examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character. (single-trailing-newline)
   200	```
   201	
   202	The format of the output for any rules that are triggered is as follows:
   203	
   204	`file-name:line:column: rule-id: description (aliases)`
   205	
   206	- `file-name` - Path to the file that triggered the rule.
   207	- `line`/`column` - Position in the file where the rule was triggered.
   208	- `rule-id` - Unique identifier assigned to the rule.
   209	- `description` - Human readable description of the rule.
   210	- `aliases` - One or more aliases used to reference the rule.
   211	
   212	For the rule violation that was reported at the start of this section,
   213	the first step in diagnosing
   214	that violation is to look at the file `/examples/example-1.md` at the end of
   215	line 3, which is column 16.  Rule [md047](/docs/rules/rule_md047.md) specifies
   216	that every file should end with a single newline character, which is
   217	what is reported in the violation's description.  Additionally, it reports that this
   218	rule can also be identified by the more human readable alias of
   219	`single-trailing-newline`.
   220	
   221	### Basic Configuration
   222	
   223	The most frequently used part of the configuration system is the
   224	part that enables and disables specific rules while scanning the
   225	Markdown files.  For example, if you do not like rule md047 which
   226	states that each file must end with a single newline, you can
   227	disable that rule by specifying:
   228	
   229	```shell
   230	pymarkdown -d md047 scan /examples
   231	```
   232	
   233	or:
   234	
   235	```shell
   236	pymarkdown --disable-rules md047 scan /examples
   237	```
   238	
   239	The effect of disabling the rule should be evidenced by
   240	the scan no longer reporting any violations of rule md047
   241	against the Markdown file `example-1.md`.
   242	
   243	Alternatively, rules can also be enabled.  As the modelled
   244	base rules for this project are based off those rules for David
   245	Anson's project, rule md002 is disable by default in both
   246	projects.  Specifically, rule md002 is disabled by default
   247	as rule md041 provides a better implementation of that rule
   248	that takes front-matter into account.  Until that rule is
   249	implemented, you can enable rule md002 by specifying either:
   250	
   251	```shell
   252	pymarkdown -e md002 scan /examples
   253	```
   254	
   255	or
   256	
   257	```shell
   258	pymarkdown --enable-rules md002 scan /examples
   259	```
   260	
   261	The effect of enabling the rule is evidenced by
   262	the scan reporting a violation of Rule md002 against
   263	Markdown file `example-1.md`:
   264	
   265	```text
   266	examples/example-1.md:1:1: MD002: First heading of the document should be a top level heading.
   267	 [Expected: h1; Actual: h2] (first-heading-h1, first-header-h1)
   268	examples/example-1.md:3:16: MD047: Each file should end with a single newline character.
   269	(single-trailing-newline)
   270	```
   271	
   272	### Extensions
   273	
   274	Extensions are any features that are implemented in addition to the base
   275	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/) specification.
   276	These extensions are documented in the [extensions](/docs/extensions.md)
   277	document, including information about how they perform, details on the
   278	extension, and configuration information.

   301	## Open Issues and Future Plans
   302	
   303	During the development phase of this project, it was more useful to have
   304	an actual list of issues to track and prioritize, rather than relying on
   305	GitHub to do all the work. This is the location of the prioritized
   306	[Issues List](/issues.md).
   307	
   308	If you find any issues, please report them using the standard GitHub
   309	issues process.  When our team looks at your issue and triages
   310	it, it will be added to our Issues List with the triaged priority.
   311	For us, this provides transparency as to what we are currently working
   312	on, what is up next, and what our plans are for further development.

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

            source_markdown == original_markdown
        ), f"Markdown strings are not equal.{diff_values}"

[Command finished with exit code 1]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see the issue clearly. The debug output shows:

**Expected:** `   >    >    1. list\n        >       item` (8 spaces before `>`)
**Actual:** `   >    >    1. list\n                >       item` (16 spaces before `>`)

The issue is in the transform process where the second line is getting double the indentation it should have. Looking at the debug trace, I can see:
- Line 1: `"   >    >    1. list"` (correct)
- Line 2: `"                >       item"` (wrong - should be `"        >       item"`)

The key insight from the debug trace is:
```
looking for nested block start
nested_block_start_index->1:previous_token=[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]
token_stack[-1]=[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True
-->container_line>                >       item
```

This suggests the issue is in the `TransformToMarkdown` class where it's doubling the leading spaces for continuation lines.

    62	def write_temporary_configuration(supplied_configuration):
    63	    """
    64	    Write the configuration as a temporary file that is kept around.
    65	    """
    66	    try:
    67	        with tempfile.NamedTemporaryFile("wt", delete=False) as outfile:
    68	            if isinstance(supplied_configuration, str):
    69	                outfile.write(supplied_configuration)
    70	            else:

    73	    except IOError as this_exception:
    74	        raise AssertionError(

   132	def assert_token_consistency(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
   133	    """
   134	    Compare the markdown document against the tokens that are expected.
   135	    """
   136	    verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens)
   137	    # verify_line_and_column_numbers(source_markdown, actual_tokens)

   139	
   140	def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
   141	    """
   142	    Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
   143	    to the original Markdown that created the token.
   144	    """
   145	
   146	    if "\t" in source_markdown:
   147	        new_source = []
   148	        split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
   149	        for next_line in split_source:
   150	            if "\t" in next_line:
   151	                next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
   152	            new_source.append(next_line)
   153	        source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)
   154	
   155	    transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
   156	    original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)
   157	
   158	    print(
   159	        "".join(
   160	            [
   161	                "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
   162	                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
   163	                "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
   164	                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
   165	                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
   166	            ]
   167	        )
   168	    )
   169	    diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
   170	    diff_values = "".join(
   171	        [
   172	            "\n-=-=-n",
   173	            ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
   174	            "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
   175	            ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
   176	            "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
   177	            ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
   178	            "<--\n-=-=-\n",
   179	        ]
   180	    )
   181	
   182	    assert (
   183	        source_markdown == original_markdown
   184	    ), f"Markdown strings are not equal.{diff_values}"

        actual_lines = original_markdown.split('\n')

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Let me analyze the issue more carefully:

From the debug output, here's what I can see:

1. The input is: `   >    >    1. list\n        >       item`
2. The first line correctly gets processed as: `   >    >    1. list`
3. The second line should remain as: `        >       item` (8 spaces)
4. But it's getting transformed to: `                >       item` (16 spaces)

Looking at the debug trace:
```
1(2)>>container_line>>21-41:>:        >       item:<
(21)
(3):(True, 0, '[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]')
(3):(True, 0, '[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]')
 -->did_move_ahead>False
 -->['[block-quote(1,4):   :   > ]', '[block-quote(1,9)::   >    > \n]', '[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]']
 -->[0, 1, 0]
 -->did_move_ahead>False
 -->current_changed_record>None
 container->[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]
 -->['        ']
 -->container_line>        >       item
 -->container_line>                >       item
```

The key issue is that when `did_move_ahead` is `False`, the condition in `__apply_primary_transformation` evaluates to `True` for `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line`:

```python
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line = not (
    did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]
)
```

Since `did_move_ahead` is `False` and `current_changed_record` is `None`, this evaluates to `not (False and None)` which is `not False` = `True`.

Then in the block:
```python
if applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line:
    split_leading_spaces = token_stack[-1].leading_spaces.split(...)
    if last_container_token_index < len(split_leading_spaces):
        container_line = split_leading_spaces[last_container_token_index] + container_line
```

The issue is that `token_stack[-1]` is the list token `[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]` and its `leading_spaces` contains `'        '` (8 spaces). But this space is already in the `container_line`, so adding it again doubles it to 16 spaces.

The problem is that for continuation lines (second line of a paragraph that spans multiple lines), we shouldn't be adding the leading spaces from the list token because they're already present in the text.

Looking at the token structure:
- `[olist(1,14):.:1:16:   :        ]` - the last part `:        ` indicates the leading spaces for continuation lines
- But these spaces are already in the actual text line `        >       item`

So the fix should be to check if we're dealing with a continuation line and avoid double-applying the leading spaces.

The root cause is in the logic that determines when to apply leading spaces. For continuation lines in nested structures, the spaces are already embedded in the text and shouldn't be added again.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>### 5.1 Problem Statement
The issue is that in maximally nested block quote structures, the second line of list items gets **double indentation**. Specifically, 8 extra spaces are added, resulting in 16 spaces instead of the expected 8 spaces.

### 5.2 Problem Location
The problem is located in `/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py` in the `__apply_primary_transformation` method around lines 434-454.

### 5.3 How the Test Reproduces the Issue
The test creates a deeply nested structure: `block quote -> block quote -> ordered/unordered list`, where the second line of the list item doesn't have block quote markers. The transform process incorrectly applies leading spaces twice.

### 5.4 Best Practices for the Fix
- Make minimal changes to avoid breaking other functionality
- Ensure the fix doesn't affect non-nested or non-continuation line scenarios
- Preserve the existing logic for normal cases
- Test edge cases thoroughly

### 5.5 How to Fix the Problem
The issue occurs when:
1. `did_move_ahead` is `False` (indicating we're on a continuation line)
2. `current_changed_record` is `None` (no new container started)
3. The logic sets `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line = True`
4. Leading spaces from the list token are applied even though they're already present

The fix should check if we're dealing with a continuation line that already contains the proper leading spaces, and avoid applying them again.

   445	                    " -->container_line>"
   446	                    + ParserHelper.make_value_visible(container_line)
   447	                )
   448	                leading_space_to_add = split_leading_spaces[last_container_token_index]
   449	                # Check if the container line already starts with the leading space
   450	                # to avoid double-application in continuation lines
   451	                if not container_line.startswith(leading_space_to_add):
   452	                    container_line = leading_space_to_add + container_line
   453	                print(
   454	                    " -->container_line>"
   455	                    + ParserHelper.make_value_visible(container_line)
   456	                )
   457	        return (
   458	            last_container_token_index,
   459	            applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line,
   460	            container_line,
   461

Let me also test some edge cases to make sure I didn't break anything:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test_edge_cases.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3

"""
Test edge cases to make sure the fix doesn't break other scenarios.
"""

from application_properties import ApplicationProperties
from pymarkdown.extension_manager import ExtensionManager
from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown

def test_case(name, source_markdown):
    print(f"\n=== Testing {name} ===")
    print(f"Input:  {repr(source_markdown)}")
    
    try:
        # Parse the markdown
        tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
        test_properties = ApplicationProperties()
        extension_manager = ExtensionManager()
        extension_manager.initialize(None, test_properties)
        extension_manager.apply_configuration()
        tokenizer.apply_configuration(test_properties, extension_manager)
        actual_tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
        
        # Transform back
        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)
        
        print(f"Output: {repr(original_markdown)}")
        print(f"Match:  {source_markdown == original_markdown}")
        
        if source_markdown != original_markdown:
            print("DIFFERENCE DETECTED!")
            return False
        return True
        
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"ERROR: {e}")
        return False

def main():
    test_cases = [
        # Original issue cases
        ("Original ordered list issue", "   >    >    1. list\n        >       item"),
        ("Original unordered list issue", "   >    >    + list\n        >      item"),
        
        # Simpler cases
        ("Simple block quote", "> text"),
        ("Simple nested block quote", "> > text"),
        ("Simple list", "- item"),
        ("Nested list", "- - item"),
        
        # Mixed cases
        ("> - list in quote", "> - item\n>   more"),
        ("List with continuation", "- item\n  more"),
        
        # Other nested structures
        ("> > - triple nested", "> > - item\n> >   more"),
    ]
    
    passed = 0
    total = len(test_cases)
    
    for name, markdown in test_cases:
        if test_case(name, markdown):
            passed += 1
    
    print(f"\n=== Results ===")
    print(f"Passed: {passed}/{total}")
    
    if passed == total:
        print("All tests passed!")
        return True
    else:
        print("Some tests failed!")
        return False

if __name__ == "__main__":
    success = main()
    exit(0 if success else 1)</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>File created successfully at: /workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test_edge_cases.py</tool_response><|assistant|></think><tool_call>execute_bash<arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>cd /workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9 && python test_edge_cases.py</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>=== Testing Original ordered list issue ===

Let me think of a more targeted approach. The real issue is that I need to be more specific about WHEN the leading spaces are already there.

Let me look at this differently. What makes the original failing case special?

Original failing case:
- Input: `   >    >    1. list\n        >       item`  
- The second line `        >       item` already has the correct leading spaces `        `
- When processing, we extract `        ` from the list token's leading_spaces
- But the container_line is already `        >       item`
- So adding `        ` + `        >       item` = `                >       item` (wrong)

Other case that should work:
- Input has different structure where leading spaces aren't pre-applied

Maybe the key is to check if we're in a "max nested" scenario AND the container line already has the leading spaces. Let me be more precise and check that the leading space we're about to add is EXACTLY the same as what's already at the start of container_line.

But I also realize there might be an even simpler approach. Let me check if the issue is specifically about when we're dealing with continuation lines that are already properly formatted.

Actually, let me step back and think about the semantics. In the original issue:
1. We have a list token with leading_spaces = `        ` (8 spaces for continuation)
2. The container_line is `        >       item` (already has 8 spaces)  
3. We don't want to add the 8 spaces again

But in the failing case:
1. We have a list token with leading_spaces = `     ` (5 spaces for continuation)  
2. The container_line is `      item` (has 6 spaces, but they're not the same pattern)
3. We DO want to add the 5 spaces

The difference might be that in the original case, the container_line starts with EXACTLY the same leading space sequence that we're about to add.

"""
Debug script to reproduce the failing case.
"""

from application_properties import ApplicationProperties
from pymarkdown.extension_manager import ExtensionManager
from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown

def test_issue():
    # Test case from the failing test
    source_markdown = """   >    +     > list
   >            item"""
    
    print("Input markdown:")
    print(repr(source_markdown))
    print()
    
    # Parse the markdown
    tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
    test_properties = ApplicationProperties()
    extension_manager = ExtensionManager()
    extension_manager.initialize(None, test_properties)
    extension_manager.apply_configuration()
    tokenizer.apply_configuration(test_properties, extension_manager)
    actual_tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
    
    print("Parsed tokens:")
    for i, token in enumerate(actual_tokens):
        print(f"{i}: {repr(token)}")
    print()
    
    # Transform back to markdown using TransformToMarkdown (the one causing issues)
    transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
    original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)
    
    print("Transformed markdown:")
    print(repr(original_markdown))
    print()
    
    print("Comparison:")
    print(f"Expected: {repr(source_markdown)}")
    print(f"Actual:   {repr(original_markdown)}")
    print(f"Equal:    {source_markdown == original_markdown}")
    
    if source_markdown != original_markdown:
        print("\nDifference analysis:")
        expected_lines = source_markdown.split('\n')
        actual_lines = original_markdown.split('\n')
        
        for i, (exp, act) in enumerate(zip(expected_lines, actual_lines)):
            if exp != act:
                print(f"Line {i}: Expected {repr(exp)}, got {repr(act)}")

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

```
3(4)>>container_line>>21-28:>:   item:<
...
container->[olist(2,3):.:1:5::     \n   ]
-->['     ', '   ']
-->container_line>   item
-->container_line>   item
...
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line->True
-->container_line>   item
-->container_line>>    item
```

Here:
- `container_line` = `   item` (3 spaces)  
- `leading_space_to_add` = `   ` (3 spaces from split_leading_spaces[1])
- Since `   item` STARTS with `   `, my condition prevents adding → CORRECT!

But then something else adds one more space to make it `>    item` instead of `>       item`.

The issue is that there are TWO levels of processing happening:
1. My fix prevents double-addition 
2. Something else is supposed to add the correct amount

But in the original failing case, we needed to prevent the addition, and in this case, we also correctly prevented it, but then the downstream processing isn't adding enough.

This suggests that my fix is actually working correctly for the specific issue, but there might be other parts of the system that need adjustment.
